1.0   
INTRODUCTION 
The impetus of the Parsley Boulevard Corridor Study is in response to Wyoming Department of 
Transportation’s (WYDOT) need to replace the bridge over Interstate 80 that has been hit by oversized 
loads on Interstate 80. The bridge has been hit and repaired numerous times over the last several years. 
WYDOT has programed the re-construction of the bridge for the 2022 construction season. As the 
Interstate 80 crossing is approximately halfway from the Ames Avenue intersection to the north and 
College Drive on the south. The corridor study is needed to create a comprehensive plan to optimize safety 
growth, and fiscal responsibility. 
The goal of the study is three fold, first to understand the community, businesses and neighborhoods 
vision for the roadway, second to improve roadway and intersection safety and function that encourages 
and accommodates all forms of transportation (i.e., industrial traffic, motor vehicles, bicycles, and 
pedestrians), third to guide the design development for the new WYDOT Interstate 80 bridge structure to 
be replaced in fiscal year 2022. 
Study Area 
Parsley Boulevard Corridor: 
1. Ames Avenue to College Drive and 
College Drive to Wallick Road.  
Key Intersections: 
2. 4th Street and Parsley Boulevard. 
3. 3rd Street and Parsley Boulevard. 
4. Jefferson Road and Parsley 
Boulevard. 
5. Pinto Lane and Parsley Boulevard. 
6. College Drive and Parsley Boulevard. 
The study includes 35% design of Parsley 
Boulevard from Ames Avenue to College 
Drive. A conceptual alignment of Parsley 
Boulevard is included from College Drive to 
West Wallick Road.

REVIEW OF EXISTING INFORMATION 
This section of the plan provides a summary of the existing roadway and planning area.  
History 
Based on the Cheyenne-Laramie County Cooperative Geographic Information System (GIS) database 
search/interactive mapping site, the first recorded plat of Parsley Boulevard is the Interior Heights addition 
in 1954 (Figure 2-1). The plat recorded the roadway as Branch County Road No.14.  A 1949 plat by the 
Union Pacific Railroad of a portion of SE ¼ and S ½ of NE ¼ Section 1, T13N, R67W, 6th P.M. calls out a 
county road on the section line between Section 1, T13N, R67W and Section 6, T13N, R66W. However, there 
is no dimension called out for the width of the right-of-way. This same plat depicts a “Road” which would 
appear to be the portion of current Parsley Boulevard from Pacific Avenue to Ames Avenue (Figure 2-2). 
The Final Plat of David Romero South Cheyenne Community Park which was recorded in 2008 depicts 
Parsley Boulevard as 80’ right-of-way from approximately Pacific Avenue to Ames Avenue and a 100’ right-
of-way centered on the section line common to Section 1 T13N, R67W and Section 6, T13N, R66W. There is 
also a call out on this plat of 120’ Right-of-Way (Figure 2-4). There appears to be a discrepancy between 
the various plats of the width of Parsley Boulevard right-of-way from Unicover Drive to Pacific Avenue 
(north). This should be investigated further as part of the improvement design. 
Historical Review 
The Parsley Boulevard Corridor plan area is not known to be a part of any historic districts at the present 
time.  Additionally, the Wyoming State Historic Preservation Office (SHPO) website was reviewed for all the 
National Register listings in the area of the study and none were found.   
Please note that if federal funds are used on any future projects or if a federal agency is part of the 
planning and implementation, a Section 106 Study will be required to determine potential impacts to any 
historic properties.  Properties in the area of any construction impacts will be identified and evaluated 
based on the Secretary of Interior’s Standards and Guidelines for identification.  Several determinations can 
be made in the evaluation including the following: 
 
No historic properties affected, 
 
Historic property adversely affected, 
 
Historic property not adversely affected.

Existing Corridor 
Today the Parsley Boulevard Corridor from Ames Avenue 
to College Drive is comprised of a two-lane rural section 
with minimal shoulders, no sidewalks or pedestrian paths 
exist. The corridor is fully developed on the east with 
David R. Romero Park and urban subdivisions, zoned 
Medium Density Residential. The west side of the corridor 
has been developed and zoned Light Industrial. Figure 
2-5, Figure 2-6 and Figure 2-8. 
There is a portion of Parsley Boulevard from West 
Jefferson Road to Sundance Lane where residents on the 
east side have been using the Parley Boulevard right-of-
way as parking and access to properties Figure 2-7.  
Parsley Boulevard South Of College Drive to 
Proposed Wallick Road. 
This section of the conceptual alignment of Parsley 
Boulevard is bounded by four (4) private property owners 
three (3) of which is zoned A-1, Agriculture and Rural 
Residential and the fourth is zoned PUD. There is an 
easement on the Sara property for the Board of Public 
Utilities (BOPU) transmission main. There is an existing 
Black Hills Energy town border station gas facility directly 
south of the Parsley Boulevard/College Drive intersection 
(Figure 2-9).

Drainage 
Most all of Parsley Boulevard is within the Clear Creek drainage basin. Clear Creek crosses Parsley Boulevard 
just south of Pacific Avenue adjacent the Dave Romero Park. The FEMA 100-year Floodplain overtops 
Parsley Boulevard at approximately one-foot depth. Refer to Figure 2-14. The 1988 Drainage Master Plan, 
by CH2MHill called for (3) 10’ x 6’ reinforced concrete box culverts to be installed at the Clear Creek 
crossing.  
Future improvements to Parsley Boulevard including an urban street section will require significant 
drainage analysis and design of storm water conveyance systems to collect and convey storm water to 
existing drainage facilities. 
 
 
 
Figure 2-14 - Floodplain

Bicycle / Pedestrian Transportation 
Currently there are no bicycle / pedestrian facilities within the corridor. The existing leg of the Cheyenne 
Greenway under the WAPA Powerline Easement ends at Parsley Boulevard just south of West Jefferson 
Road.

Transit 
The Cheyenne Transit has one designated stop on Parsley Boulevard at West Jefferson Road. The program 
runs 6:00 am – 7:00 pm Monday thru Friday and 10:00 am – 5:00 pm on Saturday. Based on the current 
route map, the departure time from the stop is thirty-five (35) minutes after the hour.

Environmental 
Potential Environmental considerations include three (3) site along the corridor: 
1. Parsley Boulevard Landfill. This landfill was acquired by the City of Cheyenne in 1945 and was 
thought to be used as and burn and bury dump by the Public Works Department from 1952-1968. 
2. Meadow Salvage Yard. Former junk yard site reportedly filled with unknown materials in operation 
until around 1968. 
3. Wyott Industrial Facility. Reported contamination from industrial waste from fifty-five (55) gallon 
drums.

Traffic Safety  
Crash data was provide by WYDOT and the City of Cheyenne for each of the key intersections along the 
corridor for the time period beginning on January, 2014 and ending December 2018. The number of 
crashes ranged from a total of eleven (11) at Parsley Boulevard and Ames Avenue and one (1) at the 
intersection of Parsley Boulevard and Musical Drive. Given the low calculated crash rates, it would appear 
that no crash problems are present on the corridor at this time. 
The Crash data is detailed in Appendix E and summarized in Table 2-2.

3.0   
PROJECT MEETINGS 
The Parsley Boulevard Corridor Study relied upon extensive public and stakeholder participation. The 
process involved open house format meetings with residents, business owners, developers, and land 
owners, as well as, project Steering Committee, Planning Commission, Cheyenne Metropolitan Planning  
Organization (MPO) Technical Advisory Committee, Cheyenne Metropolitan Planning Organization (MPO) 
Citizen Advisory Committee, and Individual Landowner meetings. Table 3- 1 Public Outreach Matrix shows 
all the activity and dates in the process of the project.   
The team set up several avenues for the public to provide input from meetings: 
 
Contact AVI office by phone to setup an appointment to meet with at their convenience, 
 
Contact us by email at avi@avipc.com to request at time to meet, 
 
Send your comments directly by email to avi@avipc.com,  
 
Mail or drop off your comments at AVI, Professional Corporation, 
 
Post comments to Survey Monkey® at http://www.surveymonkey.com/s/. 
Results for the written survey information were entered into the computer system by the consultant after 
the open houses and the public had the option of entering the survey electronically through the Survey 
Monkey® web link. The link was provided on the Cheyenne Metropolitan Planning Organization (MPO) 
website. A complete summary of comments, exhibits, sign in sheets, and comment cards are enclosed in 
Appendix B Public Meeting.

One-on-one Meetings  
The one-on-one individual meetings with adjacent property owners south of College Drive concluded with 
all property owners, that the best alignment for Parsley Boulevard was to generally follow the existing 
waterline alignment which can be seen in Figure 3-1 as an excavation scar on the eastern edge of the 
Joseph Sara Property.

following staff and key stakeholders from the MPO/City/County/WYDOT and other agencies:  
 
Tom Mason, Cheyenne Metropolitan Planning Organization,

The Steering Committee met three (3) times throughout the course of the project to guide the consultant 
team, review project information, provide insight, discuss public and stakeholder involvement, and 
collaborate to make decisions about the plan direction and recommendations.   
Public Open Houses  
First Open House  
The first public meeting was conducted on October 24, 2018 from 5:30 p.m. to 7:00 p.m. at the Youth 
Activities and Community Center (YACC), 1317 Parsley Boulevard, Cheyenne, WY.  The site was selected due 
to its proximity to the corridor study.  The meeting was advertised through various media including 
newspaper, Cheyenne Metropolitan Planning Organization (MPO) website, Facebook and variable message 
board signs placed on Parsley Boulevard at Ames Avenue and at Musical Drive. Thirty-four (34) people were 
listed on the sign-in-sheet as attending the meeting. The objectives of the open house were as follows: 
 
Obtain and listen to initial feedback from the public on problems, concerns, and desires for the 
future improvements and transportation components of the area. 
 
Discuss WYDOT’s plan for the bridge replacement in 2022. 
 
Provide a platform to begin work with the Steering Committee. 
Planning and Engineering consultants from AVI, MPO, and WYDOT were present to receive public 
comment.     

PARSLEY BOULEVARD CORRIDOR STUDY 
PROJECT MEETINGS

Information and input was collected using three different avenues; direct communication with a team 
member (i.e. AVI, MPO, and WYDOT staff members), having the public write comments on Post-it® notes 
and placing them on large planning area maps, and filling out a written survey.  The primary purpose of the 
three different communication avenues was to create the most comfortable environment for individuals to 
convey information to the team. Refer to Figure 3-2. 
 
 
Figure 3-2 – Open House Number One Display with input notes.

Overview 
Sixty-eight percent (68%) of respondents were Home Owner’s in the area while 26.67% were Route Users.   
The summary input from the public from the first public meeting included: 
 
The desire for Bicycle (71.43%) and Pedestrian Facilities (88%) throughout the corridor, as well as on 
the new bridge; 
 
Concerns about bridge construction and detours;  
 
Relieving congestion at peak hours and Street Lighting at the College Intersection was the third 
most common topic.  
Q3. Please Rate the importance of the following transportation users and issues base on 
what you consider to be the most important design Consideration for Parsley Blvd? 
 
Figure 3-3 – Results from Q3 
Second Open House  
The second public meeting was conducted on May 7, 2019 from 5:00 p.m. to 6:30 p.m. in the Cafeteria of 
Johnson Jr. High 1236 W. Allison Road, Cheyenne, WY.  A rolling power point show was setup to present 
facts and figures of the corridor study, WYDOT’s time for bridge work and results of public input from the 
first meeting.   
Two identical workshop areas comprised of exhibits shown in the presentation and a detailed aerial 
overview of the plan. Breaking the public into smaller groups allowed more one on one conversations and 
interactions with the public. This was an attempt to ensure that the public in attendance was allowed to

speak freely and get to know the people working on the corridor plan. A separate place was provided for 
the public to write comments and to answer a written survey.   
Thirty-five (35) people were listed on the Sign-In-Sheet as attending the meeting.   The objectives of the 
open house were as follows: 
 
Conduct a Presentation to provide background, purpose, and goals of project. 
 
Convey what we heard from the neighborhood meetings / recommendations. 
 
Convey results of the traffic study. 
 
The consultants presented different road options based on the direction of public comment 
landowner meetings and provided visual diagrams of what could be done along the corridor. 
Planning and Engineering consultants from AVI were present to receive public comment from the citizens 
that participated in the open house style meeting. MPO and WYDOT staff members assisted in fielding 
questions, gathering comments, and directing citizens to the different areas of the open house.  
Overview 
The majority of the respondents identified as home owners and route users in the area. 
Q1. Which of the following best describes you (Please mark all that apply)?  
 
Figure 3-4 – Results from Q1 
A significant majority of survey respondents were in favor of the three-lane section, curb and gutter as well 
as the pedestrian / bicycle paths, throughout the corridor from Ames Avenue to College Drive. The highest 
priority was to widen the roadway surface to accommodate shoulders followed by lighting at major 
intersections and greenway or multi-use path throughout the corridor. Refer to Appendix A Public Meeting 
for survey results. 
 

PARSLEY BOULEVARD CORRIDOR STUDY 
RECOMMENDED DESIGN ELEMENTS 
July 31, 2019 
 
 
2-4135.18 
AVI Professional Corporation 1103 Old Town Lane; Suite 101; Cheyenne, WY 82009 P| 307-637-6017 
Page | 26 
4.0  
RECOMMENDED DESIGN ELEMENTS   
Key-Planning Considerations 
This phase of the project provides a framework for the future land development and corridor vision of 
the various stakeholders.  The Parsley Boulevard Corridor from Ames Avenue to College Drive has been 
fully developed over the years with urban density on the east side of the corridor and light industrial on 
the west. The Parsley Boulevard Corridor from College Drive to Wallick Road is currently undeveloped.   
The entire corridor from Ames Avenue to College Drive is within the city limits with the exception of the 
tank farm property on the west side from West Allison to College Drive, therefore improvements to the 
corridor will be required it to be constructed to the City of Cheyenne Unified Development Code 
Standards including number of lanes, curb & gutter, parking, pedestrian and bicycle facilities. Planning 
considerations include:  
 
Provide the Bridge over I-80, with pedestrian and bicycle recommendations; 
 
Provide a safe, accessible and continuous pedestrian connection along the entire corridor;  
 
Provide street lighting at intersections and non-motorized crossings where appropriate; 
 
Provide for expanding transit stops along the corridor; 
 
Provide bike lanes as recommended by the Cheyenne Area On-Street Bicycle Plan and Greenway 
Plan Update by Alta Planning + Design in 2012 (Group, 2012).  
 
Review options to expand the Greenway for connectivity to schools and existing greenway 
components. 
Traffic Study 
A Traffic study was performed by Kimley-Horn is to evaluate traffic safety, transportation needs, and 
provide recommendations for managing vehicular traffic and promoting a multi-modal transportation 
approach along Parsley Boulevard in Cheyenne, Wyoming. 
The traffic analysis includes the evaluation of the existing and long term 2040 horizon traffic for the 
purposes of this study. In addition to applying a standardized traffic volume growth rate, trip 
generation, trip distribution, and project traffic assignment for an adjacent 700-unit residential 
development proposed at the northeast corner of Parsley Boulevard and College Drive was also 
included within the 2040 total traffic volumes.  
Conclusions and Recommendations 
Although not found to be needed to provide additional traffic capacity, it is recommended that Parsley 
Boulevard be improved to be a three-lane roadway between College Drive and Ames Avenue to provide 
left-turn lanes at all major intersections. These left turn lanes will improve safety and facilitate efficient 
traffic flow. Likewise, to provide a complete street to serve other modes of travel, it is recommended 
that the roadway be constructed with adequate width so that bicycle lanes can be designated on both 
sides of Parsley Boulevard. Curb and gutter is recommended on both sides of Parsley Boulevard. 
 
 

PARSLEY BOULEVARD CORRIDOR STUDY 
RECOMMENDED DESIGN ELEMENTS 
July 31, 2019 
 
 
2-4135.18 
AVI Professional Corporation 1103 Old Town Lane; Suite 101; Cheyenne, WY 82009 P| 307-637-6017 
Page | 27 
At the Parsley Boulevard and Interstate-80 bridge overpass it is recommended that the roadway be 
constructed with 12-foot travel lanes, 4-foot shoulders and a 10-foot pedestrian walkway on the east 
side of Parsley Boulevard so that all modes of travel can safely utilize the overpass. 
Due to the planned bridge over Interstate 80 only including sidewalk on the east side of the bridge, it is 
recommended  that the section of Parsley Boulevard  between Pacific Avenue/3rd Street and Unicover 
only include sidewalk on the east side of the street. A designated pedestrian crossing is recommended 
to be constructed on the south leg of the Pacific Avenue/3rd Street intersection and on the north leg of 
the Unicover intersection where the existing sidewalk path exists through the powerline easement.    
Ultimately these sidewalk connections will connect the residential areas to David R. Romero South 
Cheyenne Community Park. 
All on-street parking is recommended to be restricted along Parsley Boulevard. Presently, parking is 
observed to occur between Sundance Lane and the pedestrian path along the powerline easement (a 
length of approximately 1,200 feet). An access to a newly developed alleyway is proposed to provide 
access for nearby residents within this area for parking and access for the seven (7) properties with 
garages along Parsley Boulevard within this section of roadway.  Where possible, it is recommended 
that driveway accesses along the west side of Parsley Boulevard be realigned with public street 
intersections on the east side of the roadway. 
At the intersection of Parsley Boulevard and Ames Avenue, the eastbound left turn lane may need to be 
extended to include 225-foot of queue storage space in the year 2040 if future volumes are realized. 
This turn lane length can be accommodated when Parsley Boulevard is improved to be a three-lane 
roadway. Likewise, separate 150-foot left-turn lanes are recommended at all major intersections along 
Parsley Boulevard.  At the intersection of Parsley Boulevard and College Drive an eastbound 
acceleration lane from the southbound left-turn onto College Drive is recommended to improve traffic 
operations. The total recommended acceleration lane length is 760 feet (580-foot acceleration length 
plus an 80­ foot taper length). The existing striped center median is recommended to be restriped to 
include an acceleration lane.  Since College Drive is a Wyoming State Highway, it is recommended  that 
the WYDOT evaluate College Drive  for  warrants  to  install  street  lights  at  this  intersection  and  
along the College Drive corridor as appropriate.

PARSLEY BOULEVARD CORRIDOR STUDY 
PUBLIC OPEN HOUSE ONE 
June 3, 2019 
 
 
 
 
Public Open House One 
The Open House will be held on Wednesday, October 24, 
2018 from 5:30 p.m. to 7:00 p.m. in the Youth Activities and 
Community Center (YACC), 1317 Parsley Blvd. Cheyenne, WY. 

  
 
 
PARSLEY BLVD 
PUBLIC OPEN HOUSE 
 
The public is invited to attend a Public Open House for the 
Parsley Blvd Corridor Study.  The purpose of the meeting is to 
identify issues, and solicit ideas and comments.  Your input is 
important as we develop the plans for the future improvements of the 
roadway corridor. The project limits of Parsley Blvd are from Ames 
Ave. on the north to College Drive on the South.  The Open House 
will be held on Wednesday, October 24, 2018 from 5:30 p.m. to 
7:00 p.m. in the Youth Activities and Community Center (YACC), 
1317 Parsley Blvd. Cheyenne, WY.  
For more information call please call the MPO at (307) 637-6299 
 

 
 
October 19, 2018 
 
2-4135.18 
 
 
RE: 
Parsley Boulevard Corridor Study  
 
The Cheyenne Metropolitan Planning Organization (the Cheyenne area long-range transportation 
planning agency) in coordination with the City, County and WYDOT have contracted AVI, a 
Cheyenne engineering and planning firm to perform a planning study of the Parsley Boulevard 
corridor between College Drive and Ames Avenue/Deming Drive.    
The roadway condition of Parsley Boulevard is generally acceptable, but it is rural in character and 
does not accommodate future traffic volumes or have facilities which provide for other modes of 
travel or access per the Americans with Disabilities Act (ADA).  The main obstacle to the conversion 
of Parsley to an urban street is the narrow bridge structure that spans I-80 and the road does not 
have sufficient width for sidewalks, bike lanes, or large vehicles. The primary objectives of the 
project are to develop a plan for the corridor which addresses the requirements and vision which 
are defined and developed by the current and future users, surrounding neighborhood residents, 
property owners, and businesses.   The corridor will need to accommodate future traffic volume, 
as well as, multimodal vehicles types (i.e. bus, car, industrial, semi-truck and trailer, bike, 
pedestrian, etc.).    
There is no funding identified at the present time to reconstruct this corridor, however the bridge 
over I-80 is slated to be replaced by the Wyoming Department of Transportation in fiscal year 
2022.    
To begin our planning project we have setup the first of two Public Open Houses on October 
24, 2018 at the Dave Romero Neighborhood Facility from 5:30 p.m. to 7:00 p.m. for public 
comment and input.  Since you are an adjacent business and/or landowner, we want to provide 
you with another way to give your ideas and comments.  Your input is very important as it will 
have a direct impact on the decisions made about what future improvements will occur and what 
the roadway may look like.  We would like for everyone who wishes to be involved to have that 
opportunity.  Therefore, we have set up the following ways for you to provide your input: 
1. Contact Sherry Prieskorn at our office by phone at (307) 637-6017.  Set up an 
appointment for us to meet with you the week of November 12th through November 
16th.   Our availability outside this week is more restricted but, we are happy to 
accommodate where we can. 
2. Contact us by email at avi@avipc.com to request a time to meet.  Please put Parsley Blvd. 
Meeting in the subject line. 
 
3. Fill out the enclosed comment card and either  
a. Scan and send it directly by email to avi@avipc.com.  Please put Parsley Blvd. in 
the subject line or, 
b. Mail or drop off your comments at AVI, Professional Corporation at 1103 Old 
Town Lane, Cheyenne, WY  82009. 
4. Post your comments to Survey Monkey® at  
https://www.surveymonkey.com/r/RW5DM9H 
 
Additional opportunities will be available for your participation soon over the next 4-6 months.   
AVI and the MPO look forward to getting your input and working with you throughout this 
important project. 
Sincerely,  
AVI PROFESSIONAL CORPORATION 
 
Scott Cowley, P.E. 
Project Manager

PARSLEY BOULEVARD CORRIDOR STUDY 
PUBLIC OPEN HOUSE TWO 
June 3, 2019 
 
 
 
 
Public Open House Two 
The Open House will be held on Tuesday, May 7, 2019 from 
5:00 p.m. to 6:30 p.m. at Johnson Jr. High Cafeteria  
1236 W. Allison Road, Cheyenne, WY 

 
 
  
 
 
PARSLEY BLVD CORRIDOR  
PUBLIC OPEN HOUSE 
 
Cheyenne Metropolitan Planning Organization is holding 
an Open House for the Parsley Blvd Corridor Plan.  
Recommendations for the roadway, intersections and a conceptual 
alignment of Parsley Blvd., south of College Dr. will be shown. 
Tuesday, May 7, 2019 from 5:00 to 6:30 p.m.  
Johnson Jr. High Cafeteria 
 1236 W. Allison Road, Cheyenne, WY 
YOUR INPUT IS IMPORTANT 
For more information - call the MPO at (307) 638-4385

WHAT ARE THE PURPOSE AND 
GOALS OF THE PROJECT?
Purpose
Create a comprehensive plan that strives to optimize safety, 
growth, and fiscal responsibility
Goals
Understand the community, businesses, and neighborhood 
vision for the roadway
Improve roadway and intersection safety and function safely 
that encourages and accommodates all forms of transportation 
(i.e. freight, motor vehicles, bicycles, and pedestrians)
Guide the design development for the new WYDOT I-80 bridge 
structure to be replaced in Fiscal Year 2022. 
5/7/2019
PARSLEY BOULEVARD CORRIDOR PLAN
3
FOUR COMPONENTS OF THE 
PARSLEY BLVD. STUDY
Bridge Replacement Over I-80. Construction 
planned for 2022 by WYDOT
Corridor Study from Ames Av. To College Dr. (No 
current funding for any improvements)
Provide short and long-term recommendations for 
the future needs and anticipated growth of traffic 
on Parsley Boulevard. 
Parsley Future Extension South of College Dr.
5/7/2019

1
Wider road.
5/16/2019 5:09 PM
2
Turn lanes, sidewalks/bike lanes, and buffers are a major need along Parsley Boulevard.
5/16/2019 4:35 PM
3
I like that there’s a middle turn lane and a buffer for pedestrians. We get a lot of people trying to
walk or ride bikes along this roadway and it seems dangerous for them. The bridge is too narrow
and a turning lane along the whole span of roadway would make traffic flow better.
5/16/2019 3:24 PM

after school 230 -330. Speeding issues too on Pinto. Consider that
5/18/2019 8:53 PM
2
Excellent
5/16/2019 7:53 AM
3
Try to make it easy for the tanker trucks to enter and exit Parsley
5/16/2019 7:27 AM
4
Because this road is quite ling-cars have a tendency to go well over the speed limit or pass other

5/16/2019 7:23 AM

College Dr needs to be 40 mph and no compression brakes signs, put up from railroad track to

5/16/2019 7:13 AM
6
Suggest incorporating a tree planting plan water building the 10' greenway sections. replicate the

5/16/2019 7:00 AM
7
On College Drive, especially going down the hill near Parsley, semi-trucks regularly use loud jake
brakes. There are "No Engine Braking" signs on College to help prevent this nuisance. We would
like to see a sign and possibly a reduced speed on College to 40 mph (Currently it is 50 mph).
Also, we are concerned with the Harmon Valley Housing Development causing increased traffic
on College. It is already difficult to go east onto College Dr. from Parsley. With all the new houses,
we think a traffic light at that intersection will be much NEEDED! (one the houses start going in.)
5/16/2019 6:52 AM

WHAT ARE THE PURPOSE AND 
GOALS OF THE PROJECT?
Purpose
Create a comprehensive plan that strives to optimize safety, 
growth, and fiscal responsibility
Goals
Understand the community, businesses, and neighborhood 
vision for the roadway
Improve roadway and intersection safety and function safely 
that encourages and accommodates all forms of transportation 
(i.e. freight, motor vehicles, bicycles, and pedestrians)
Guide the design development for the new WYDOT I-80 bridge 
structure to be replaced in Fiscal Year 2022.

PARSLEY BLVD. STUDY
Bridge Replacement Over I-80. Construction planned 
for 2022 by WYDOT Estimate $4,104,128.00
Corridor Study from Ames Av. To College Dr. (No 
current funding for any improvements)
Provide short and long-term recommendations for the 
future needs and anticipated growth of traffic on 
Parsley Boulevard.  Estimate $5,290,000.00
Parsley Future Extension South of College Dr.

and

PARSLEY BOULEVARD CORRIDOR STUDY 
TRAFFIC ANALYSIS 
June 3, 2019 
 
 
 
 
Traffic Analysis 

 
kimley-horn.com 
4582 South Ulster Street, Suite 1500, Denver, CO 80237 
303 228 2300 
 
February 20, 2019 
 
 
Scott Cowley, P.E. 
Vice President 
AVI Professional Corporation 
1103 Old Town Lane, Suite 101 
Cheyenne, WY 82009 
 
Re: 
Cheyenne Metropolitan Planning Organization  
 
Parsley Boulevard Corridor Study - Traffic Evaluation 
 
Cheyenne, Wyoming 
 
 
 
 
 
Dear Mr. Cowley: 
 
This traffic evaluation is in support of the Parsley Boulevard Corridor Plan being prepared 
for the Cheyenne Metropolitan Planning Organization. The purpose of this study is to 
evaluate traffic safety, transportation needs, and provide recommendations for managing 
vehicular traffic and promoting a multi-modal transportation approach along Parsley 
Boulevard in Cheyenne, Wyoming.  
 
This traffic analysis includes the evaluation of the existing and long term 2040 horizon 
traffic for the purposes of this study. In addition to applying a standardized traffic volume 
growth rate, trip generation, trip distribution, and project traffic assignment for an adjacent 
700-unit residential development proposed at the northeast corner of Parsley Boulevard 
and College Drive was also included within the 2040 total traffic volumes. A vicinity map 
illustrating the location of the corridor study area is attached as Figure 1.   
 
Existing Roadway Network and Traffic Counts 
Regional connections for traffic traveling along Parsley Boulevard is provided by Interstate 
80 (I-80) and Interstate 25 (I-25). Parsley Boulevard provides primary connections between 
Ames Avenue north of I-80 and College Drive to the south of I-80.  College Drive includes 
an interchange with I-25 to the west and an interchange with I-80 to the northeast.  
 
The City of Cheyenne Metropolitan Planning Organization classifies Parsley Boulevard as 
a minor arterial roadway. Parsley Boulevard provides one through lane in each, northbound 
and southbound, with a 40 mile per hour posted speed limit through the study area. This 
traffic evaluation includes study of the following key intersections: 
• 
Ames Avenue and Parsley Boulevard 
• 
4th Street and Parsley Boulevard 
• 
3rd Street and Parsley Boulevard 
• 
Jefferson Road and Parsley Boulevard 
• 
Pinto Lane and Parsley Boulevard 
• 
College Drive and Parsley Boulevard 
 
The T-intersection of Parsley Boulevard and Ames Avenue is signalized with permitted-only 
left turn phasing on the eastbound approach. The T-intersection of Parsley Boulevard and 
4th Street is unsignalized with stop control on the westbound 4th Street approach. The 

 Mr. Cowley

intersection of Parsley Boulevard with 3rd Street is unsignalized with stop control on the 
eastbound and westbound 3rd Street approaches. The T-intersection of Parsley Boulevard 
and Jefferson Road is unsignalized with stop control on the westbound Jefferson Road 
approach.  A separate southbound left turn lane has been constructed and designated 
along Parsley Boulevard at this Jefferson Road intersection. The T-intersection of Parsley 
Boulevard and Pinto Lane is unsignalized with stop control on the westbound Pinto Lane 
approach. The T-intersection of Parsley Boulevard and College Drive is unsignalized with 
stop control on the southbound Parsley Boulevard approach. A separate eastbound left 
turn lane has been constructed and designated along College Drive for left turns to 
northbound Parsley Boulevard.  The existing lane configurations and control of the corridor 
study intersections is provided in attached Figure 2. 
 
Existing peak hour turning movement counts were conducted at the intersection of Parsley 
Boulevard and Ames Avenue on Tuesday, February 12, 2019 during morning, midday, and 
afternoon peak hours. Additionally, existing peak hour turning movement counts were 
conducted at all other study key intersections on Wednesday, September 19, 2018 during 
morning, midday, and afternoon peak hours. The weekday counts were conducted in 15-
minute intervals during the morning and afternoon peak hours of adjacent street traffic from 
7:00 AM to 9:00 AM, 11:00 AM to 1:00 PM, and 3:00 PM to 6:00 PM.  Existing turning 
movement counts are summarized in attached Figure 3.  The raw data count sheets are 
attached with this letter as well.  
 
Future Traffic Volume Growth 
The City of Cheyenne Transportation Plan identifies a growth assumption of 1.25 percent 
per year through year 2040. Based on this, an annual growth rate of 1.25 percent per year 
was used to calculate 2040 traffic volumes at the study area intersections. The associated 
growth assumption is attached.  In addition, specific project traffic anticipated from a 700-
home residential neighborhood to be constructed along the north side of College Drive, east 
of Parsley Boulevard was included.  For purposes of this study, it was assumed that the 
neighborhood with have a direct access along Allison Road to the north and College Drive to 
the south.  In addition, it was assumed that Cribbon Avenue would be extended to the south 
to provide another connection into the residential neighborhood.   
 
Site-generated traffic estimates are determined through a process known as trip generation. 
Rates and equations are applied to a specific land use to estimate traffic generated by any 
specific development. The acknowledged source for trip generation rates is the Trip 
Generation Manual1 published by the Institute of Transportation Engineers (ITE). ITE has 
established trip rates in nationwide studies of similar land uses. For this study, Kimley-Horn 
used the ITE Trip Generation Report fitted curve equations that apply to Single-Family 
Detached Housing (ITE 210) for traffic associated with the nearby adjacent residential 
development anticipated. Midday trip generation rates were calculated based on the percent 
of daily traffic occurring during a 60-minute time period found in Appendix A of the Trip 
Generation Manual. The highest reported percent of daily traffic for Single-Family Detached 
Housing (ITE 210) between the midday hours of 11 am to 1 pm was found to be 6.1 percent. 
This percentage of the average weekday volume determined for the midday peak hour 
volume to be approximately 380 vehicle trips. Finally, the morning and afternoon directional 
                                                   
 
1 Institute of Transportation Engineers, Trip Generation Manual, Tenth Edition, Washington DC, 2017.  

 Mr. Cowley

303 228 2300 
 
distributions provided were used to estimate directional distributions for midday traffic. The 
average between these two equations would identify approximately 44 percent enter into the 
development and 56 percent exit out of the proposed neighborhood during the midday peak 
hour.  
 
Project generated traffic volumes are identified on a weekday daily as well as on a morning 
peak hour, midday hour, and afternoon peak hour basis. The morning peak hour is the 
highest one-hour time period of adjacent street traffic during four consecutive 15-minute 
intervals during the morning rush hour, between 7:00 am and 9:00 am. The midday peak 
hour is the highest one-hour time period of adjacent street traffic during four consecutive 15-
minute intervals during the morning rush hour between 11:00 am and 1:00 pm. The 
afternoon peak hour is the highest one-hour time period of four consecutive 15-minute 
intervals between the hours of 4:00 pm and 6:00 pm representing the afternoon rush hour.

residential neighborhood development. The trip generation worksheet is attached.

As summarized in the table, the single-family detached housing project proposed adjacent 
to Parsley Boulevard is anticipated to generate 6,230 weekday daily trips, of which 502 trips 
would occur during the morning peak hour, 380 would occur during the midday peak hour, 
and 658 trips would occur during the afternoon peak hour.  
 
Distribution of this residential development traffic was based on the area street system 
characteristics, existing traffic patterns and volumes, and the proposed access system for 
the project. The distribution of traffic is a means to quantify the percentage of site-generated 
traffic that approaches the site from a given direction and departs the site back to the 
original source. It is anticipated that only a portion of traffic related to the proposed 
residential development will travel along Parsley Boulevard. Figure 4 illustrates the 
expected trip distribution for the anticipated adjacent residential development with respect to 
the Parsley Boulevard Corridor Study. Traffic assignment was obtained by applying the 
project trip distribution from Figure 4 to the estimated traffic generation of the residential 
neighborhood development shown in the trip generation table. The traffic assignment is 
shown in Figure 5. These residential neighborhood traffic volumes were added to the 2040 
background volumes to represent estimated long-term traffic conditions. The 2040 total 
traffic volumes are illustrated in Figure 6.

Traffic Operations Analysis  
Kimley-Horn’s analysis of traffic operations was conducted to determine potential capacity 
deficiencies at the study area key intersections for the 2040 study horizon for the Parsley 
Road Corridor Study. The acknowledged source for determining overall capacity is the 
Highway Capacity Manual2.  
 
Capacity analyses were performed during the weekday morning, midday, and afternoon 
peak hours for the existing roadway configuration and proposed improved configuration in 
the 2040 future year horizon.  It is recommended that Parsley Boulevard be constructed as 
a three-lane roadway with left turn lanes constructed and designated at the public street 
intersections and major accesses along the corridor. Capacity analysis results are listed in 
terms of Level of Service (LOS). LOS is a qualitative term describing operating conditions a 
driver will experience while traveling on a particular street or highway during a specific time 
interval. It ranges from A (very little delay) to F (long delays and congestion). For 
intersections and roadways in this study area, typical traffic study practice identifies overall 
intersection LOS D and movement or approach LOS E as the minimum thresholds for 
acceptable operations.  The following Table 2 shows the definition of level of service for 
signalized and unsignalized intersections based on the amount of average delay 
experienced by each vehicle.

The following provides a discussion of the operational analysis results of the key study area 
intersections on an intersection by intersection basis.  Operational analysis was performed 
for existing traffic with the existing intersection configuration and control and for the future 
2040 traffic with the recommended three-lane Parsley Boulevard roadway configuration.  
 
Parsley Boulevard and Ames Avenue 
The T-intersection of Parsley Boulevard and Ames Avenue is signalized with permitted-only 
left turn phasing on the eastbound approach. Table 3 provides the results of the level of 
service at the Parsley Boulevard and Ames Avenue intersection. With the existing 
intersection lane configuration and control, all movements currently operate acceptably 
during the morning, midday, and afternoon peak hours. With this configuration and 
signalized control, the capacity analysis indicates that acceptable delay and level of service 
is forecasted for all movements during peak hours in the long term 2040 horizon.

Parsley Boulevard and College Drive LOS 
The T-intersection of Parsley Boulevard and College Drive is unsignalized with stop control 
on the southbound approach. With the existing intersection lane configuration and control, 
all movements currently operate acceptably during the morning, midday, and afternoon peak 
hours. As stated previously, it is recommended that Parsley Boulevard be improved as a 
three-lane roadway, which would include separate left turn and right turn lanes on the 
southbound Parsley Boulevard approach to College Drive.  Additional improvements include 
an eastbound acceleration lane from the southbound left-turn onto College Drive. The total 
recommended acceleration lane length is 760 feet (580-foot acceleration length plus an 80-
foot taper length). A striped center median currently exists at the east leg of this intersection 
and is recommended to be restriped to include an acceleration lane.

acceptably during peak hours in 2040.  Table 7 provides the results of the level of service at

Queueing Analysis  
A queuing analysis was also conducted for the study area intersections with the proposed 
Parsley Boulevard improvements to include separate turn lanes at the public street 
intersections. Turn lanes are recommended to be constructed/designated to City of 
Cheyenne standards providing the recommended storage length based on the queuing 
analysis.  Results were obtained from the 95th percentile queue lengths obtained from the 
Synchro analysis. Results are shown in the following Table 8 with calculations provided 
within the level of service operational sheets for the unsignalized intersections and the 
queueing operational sheets for signalized intersections.

As shown in the table, the projected queues fall within the existing storage lengths for the 
existing southbound left turn lane at the Jefferson Road/Parsley Boulevard intersection and 
eastbound left turn at the College Drive/Parsley Boulevard intersection in the long term 2040 
horizon. Therefore, no modifications to these two left turn lanes are recommended.  At the 
intersection of Parsley Boulevard and Ames Avenue, the eastbound left turning lane may 
need to be extended to include 225-foot of queue storage space in the year 2040 if future 
volumes are realized.  This turn lane length can be accommodated when Parsley Boulevard 
is improved to be a three-lane roadway.  Likewise, separate 150-foot left-turn lanes are 
recommended at all major intersections along Parsley Boulevard.  
 
Conclusions and Recommendations  
Although not found to be needed to provide additional traffic capacity, it is recommended 
that Parsley Boulevard be improved to be a three-lane roadway between College Drive and 
Ames Avenue to provide left-turn lanes at all major intersections. These left turn lanes will 
improve safety and facilitate efficient traffic flow.  Likewise, to provide a complete street to 
serve other modes of travel, it is recommended that the roadway be constructed with 
adequate width so that bicycle lanes can be designated on both sides of Parsley Boulevard.  
Curb and gutter is recommended on both sides of Parsley Boulevard.  
 
At the Parsley Boulevard and Interstate-80 bridge overpass it is recommended that the 
roadway be constructed with 12-foot travel lanes, 4-foot shoulders and a 10-foot pedestrian 
walkway on the east side of Parsley Boulevard so that all modes of travel can safely utilize 
the overpass.  New separated sidewalks are recommended to be constructed on both sides 
of Parsley Boulevard throughout the project study area.

TRANSPORTATION PLAN: CITY OF CHEYENNE VERSION - 53
Shape
INTRODUCTION
The shape section of the Transportation Plan outlines a
transportation vision for the Cheyenne Area. The
Transportation Vision defines the roadway, transit,
bicycle, and pedestrian facilities that will be needed to
provide Cheyenne Area residents with an adequate,
connected, multimodal transportation system.
The Transportation Plan is based on information
available at the time it was created, including other
sections of PlanCheyenne. It includes enhancements to
reflect the myriad of plans conducted since the Plan
was first adopted in 2006. As future plans, documents,
and studies are developed, these studies may amend
the Transportation Plan. It is also anticipated that large
tracts of property could develop their own master
development plan. As part of the master development
plan process, transportation elements of PlanCheyenne
may be considered for amendment, provided that the
transportation elements will continue to meet the
principles, policies, and process described in
PlanCheyenne. Furthermore, priorities presented in this
plan may change in the future as development occurs.
PRINCIPLES AND POLICIES
Creation of a robust and effective transportation
system in Cheyenne requires a vision of the type of
transportation system the area desires. PlanCheyenne
lays out seven Foundations, one of which speaks
directly to transportation. The Cheyenne Area will
continue to celebrate and enhance the character,
quality, and authenticity of the community by
developing a connected and diverse transportation
system. To guide this vision, a set of principles and
policies was developed. These principles reflect a
transportation system. The associated policies present
a way to implement this vision.
GROWTH IN THE REGION
The first step in the definition of a Transportation
Vision is to identify the growth that is expected to take
place in and around Cheyenne. Growth forecasts were
generated for 2040 and beyond based on the Future
Land Use Plan. Once growth has been quantified,
future needs can be assessed.
NEEDS ASSESSMENT
After growth forecasts have been developed, the next
step in developing a Transportation Vision is to identify
needs that will arise as the region grows. These needs
include roadway needs, transit needs, and needs for
non-motorized transportation. Understanding the
needs that the community will face allows planners to
propose solutions that will fill these needs.
VISION PLANS 2040 AND BEYOND
The 2040 Transportation Vision is a fiscally
unconstrained plan for the transportation system in
the Cheyenne area. This plan provides sufficient
capacity to accommodate growth on most roadways
and includes new roadways, sidewalks, and bike lanes
in developing areas. Recommendations for retrofitting
existing roads with sidewalks and bike lanes are also
provided. The 2040 Vision Plan is based on a growth
assumption of 1.25% per year.
The Buildout Transportation Vision Plan compliments
the buildout of the Future Land Use Plan, but is not
likely to occur until sometime after 2060. The buildout
plan designates roadways and multimodal corridors
that should be preserved for future use.

Site Level of Service (LOS) Method: Delay & v/c (HCM 2010). Site LOS Method is specified in the Parameter Settings dialog (Site tab).
Roundabout LOS Method: Same as Sign Control.
Vehicle movement LOS values are based on average delay and v/c ratio (degree of saturation) per movement.
LOS F will result if v/c > 1 irrespective of movement delay value (does not apply for approaches and intersection).
Intersection and Approach LOS values are based on average delay for all movements (v/c not used as specified in HCM 2010).
Roundabout Capacity Model: US HCM 2010.
HCM Delay Formula option is used. Control Delay does not include Geometric Delay since Exclude Geometric Delay option applies.
Gap-Acceptance Capacity: Traditional M1.
HV (%) values are calculated for All Movement Classes of All Heavy Vehicle Model Designation.

# SOURCE_FILE: CHEY-2019-08-26_TotalExtraction_39653110.pdf
Approved as to
form ony:
RESOLUTION NO.
Date: btJj
ENTITLED: “A
RESOLUTION
SUPPORTING
THE
CITY
OF
CHEYENNE
TO
COMPLETE
THE
AVENUES
GREENWAY
CONNECTOR
TRAIL
BETWEEN EVANS AND AIRPORT PARKWAY AND SUPPLEMENT
AVAILABLE
WYDOT
TAP
GRANTS
WITH
6T11
PENNY
TAX
RECEIPTS.”
WHEREAS, the Avenues Greenway Connector Trail ranks number one on the public’s
preference to improve connectivity between the Eastridge and the Avenues neighborhoods; and
WHEREAS, this connectivity requires an amendment to the existing agreement with the
Wyoming Department of Transportation (WYDOT) to reallocate an existing Transportation
Alternatives Program (TAP) Grant to purchase land from the airport and extend the project
schedule; and
WhEREAS, the City will reimburse WYDOT’s right-of-way and aeronautics divisions
for the land with the TAP Grant; and
WHEREAS, the City will apply for a $500,000.00 TAP Grant as its top priority in 2020
and commit to completing the project within the next five years; and
WHEREAS, the project meets all engineering safety and FAA criteria; and
WHEREAS, the total estimated cost for this project is between $750,000.00 and
$1,000,000.00, of which $500,000.00, if approved, will be provided through the WYDOT TAP
Grant; and
WHEREAS, the required funding needed beyond the TAP Grant to complete the design
and construction of the trail segment will be provided from 6th Penny Greenway funds.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED BY THE GOVERNING BODY OF THE
CITY OF CHEYENNE, WYOMING, that 1) the Governing Body of the City of Cheyenne,
Wyoming, supports the reallocation ofthe current TAP Grant from construction to land acquisition
for the purpose of completing this project; and 2) the Mayor is authorized to sign an application
for the upcoming 2020 WYDOT TAP Grant; and 3) that the Mayor is authorized to execute an
amendment to the existing agreement with WYDOT and coordinate with to supplement the
awarded TAP Grant with 6th Penny Greenway funds.
PRESENTED, READ, AND ADOPTED THIS
DAY OF SEPTEMBER, 2019.
Marian J. Orr, Mayor
(Seal)
ATTEST:
Kristina F. Jones, City Clerk
Avenues Greenway Grant Reallocation.doe

# SOURCE_FILE: CHEY-2019-08-26_TotalExtraction_68602566.pdf
Approved as to
form only:
RESOLUTION NO.
Date: iI) 14t6j
ENTITLED: “A RESOLUTION ACCEPTING A GRANT OF $112,500.00 FROM THE
RECREATION MILL
BOARD
FOR THE
RENOVATION
OF
THE
JAYCEE PARK TENNIS COURTS.”
WHEREAS, the Cheyenne Community Recreation District receives funds from the
recreation mill levy pursuant to § W.S. 18-9-201(b); and
WhEREAS, up to 15% of the funds are available competitively to governmental
recreation organizations for public recreation projects; and
WHEREAS, the City of Cheyenne has applied for and been awarded a grant of
$112,500.00 from the Recreation Mill Board for the Jaycee Park Tennis Courts, (“Courts”); and
WHEREAS, the two existing “Courts” have a crumbling base, cracks in the playing
surface and uneven areas that collect puddles of water; and
WHEREAS, an in-kind match estimated at $5,625.00 will come from the City’s
administration of the project; and
WhEREAS, the renovation of the “Courts” will begin in the fall of 2019, weather
permitting.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED BY THE GOVERNING BODY OF THE
CITY OF CHEYENNE, WYOMING, that the Governing Body of the City of Cheyenne,
Wyoming is in support of this project and of the acceptance of this grant; and that the Mayor is
authorized to execute a grant agreement to receive this grant funding for Jaycee Park Tennis Courts
Project.
PRESENTED, READ, AND ADOPTED THIS
DAY OF SEPTEMBER 2019.
Marian J. Orr, Mayor
(Seal)
ATTEST:
Kristina F. Jones, City Clerk
TennisCrtResRecMiflGrant2Ol9.doc

Jaycee Park Tennis Courts Restoration
Recreation Mill Grant
Need
The city of Cheyenne has just twelve regulation-size tennis courts. Two ofthese tennis courts are in
Jaycee Park and are in is very poor condition, with irreparable crumbling court base, many fissures
throughout the play surface, and ponding water. In addition, the chain-link fence around the tennis
courts has become damaged and stretched out over the years, with the bottom fence fabric no longer
securely connecting with the ground.
These conditions make play on these courts less than a
positive experience.
With the current condition of these highly-visible tennis courts, serious tennis players will not use
them. Further, the cracks and dips on the surface of the courts create a potential tripping hazard.
We would like to make the repairs to this unusable tennis courts to put them back into use.
The fenced tennis courts in Jaycee Park, are in an older neighborhood with smaller, quaint homes.
The neighborhood park is 2.2 acres in size. It offers large shade trees, a playground, a basketball
court, a drinking fountain and irrigated grass. It has a very pleasant atmosphere.
Resolution to Need
We propose renovating the two tennis courts at Jaycee Park, in the Avenues neighborhood of
Cheyenne. The court resurfacing and the associated repairs will make these tennis courts useable
again.
The proposed project includes removing the chain-link fence and replacing it with new fence. The
surface of the tennis courts is to be scraped, with lose surface material removed and a paving
fabric is to be applied over the entire court. An overlay of two inches of hot-mix asphalt will then
follow. One coat of RSS acrylic resurfacer will then be applied, followed by two coats of RSS
Acrylic Color System. The court layout will be marked, and a two-inch white textured line will be
painted on the court. New tennis net posts will be installed, and new nets will be hung. Minor
traffic control will be provided.
Project equipment and cost
The estimated cost for the tennis courts includes the clean-up and removal ofmaterials from the two
existing tennis courts, new tennis court materials and installation, new tennis court posts and nets
and installation, and the application of a white textured painted line. Minor traffic control is also
included. A price quote of $112,500, from “renner” follows:

$ 52,000
Tennis Court Paving
$ 10,200
Surfacing
$
9,000
Fence
$ 41,300
Installation
$
5,625
Administration
$118,125
Total
We have been awarded $112,500 from the Cheyenne Community Public Recreation District Board
(Rec. Board) for this project. The City will be contributing $5,625 in the form of in-kind service
to this project, via project administration.

# SOURCE_FILE: CHEY-2019-08-26_TotalExtraction_18664547.pdf
Approved as to
form Only:
Date:
RESOLUTION NO.
ENTITLED: “A RESOLUTION ACCEPTING A HAZARD MITIGATION GRANT,
FROM THE WYOMING OFFICE OF HOMELAND SECURITY, IN THE
AMOUNT OF $191,430.85 FOR DRY CREEK DRAINAGE PLANNING.”
WhEREAS, the City of Cheyenne applied for and has been awarded a grant of
$191,430.85 from the Wyoming Homeland Security Office for Dry Creek Drainage Planning; and
WhEREAS, a required match has been satisfied with completed drainage work provided
by the City Drainage Engineer.
The drainage work includes hydrology work valued at
$149,026.00 and hydraulics work valued at $71,682.00; with a total match of $220,708.00; and
WHEREAS, the period of performance for the grant is May 28, 2019 through June 5,
2021; and
WHEREAS, an updated plan will provide valuable information for planning mitigation
and to avoid in the Dry Creek Basin.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED BY THE GOVERNING BODY OF THE
CITY OF CHEYENNE, WYOMING, that the Governing Body of the City of Cheyenne,
Wyoming is in support of this project and of the acceptance of this grant; and that the Mayor is
authorized to execute a grant agreement to receive this grant funding for this drainage planning
project.
PRESENTED, READ, AND ADOPTED THIS
DAY OF SEPTEMBER 2019.
Marian J. Orr, Mayor
(Seal)
ATTEST:
Kristina F. Jones, City Clerk

Dear Mayor Orr,
Mark Gordon
Governor
I wish to congratulate you on the City of Cheyenne’s successful application for Hazard Mitigation Grant
Program funding! You will find enclosed the grant award agreement for the requested grant funds. The
funding is awarded for development of the City of Cheyenne’s Dry Creek Drainage Plan.
This grant has a match requirement which you agreed to during the application process, and a maximum
federal portion of $191,430.85.00.
Please sign the enclosed grant award agreement and return it to this office.
I will then complete the
signature page and return a copy of the fully executed agreement to you.
Please feel free to contact Melinda Gibson, State Hazard Mitigation Officer at 307.777.4914 if you have
questions or comments.
Sincerely,

1.
Parties.
The parties to this Grant Award Agreement (Grant) are Wyoming Office of

Wyoming $2002, and City of Cheyenne (Subrecipient), whose address is: 2101 O’Neil

2.
Purpose of Grant. The purpose of this Grant is to set forth the terms and conditions by
which the Subrecipient shall update the 1988 Dry Creek Drainage Plan to reflect risk to
life and property resulting from new developments which effect the drainage. The goal
of the plan is to gather information necessary to identif’ and prioritize drainage
mitigation projects which minimize negative impacts of current drainage problems and to
inform future decisions on proposed development which minimize risk to life and
property from poor drainage.
3.

Date). The term of the Grant is from Effective Date through June 5, 2021. All services
shall be completed during this term.

# SOURCE_FILE: CHEY-2019-08-26_TotalExtraction_22187461.pdf
Replacement of Fire Station #5
Fire Station #5 is in North Cheyenne and services the Dell Range Blvd commercial corridor.
The Fire Station is 56 years old (constructed in 1963) and has numerous problems, as it is no
longer able to adequately serve Cheyenne’s citizens. Problems from sheer age and wear have
found this station unfit for continued service.
There has been expansive growth in North Cheyenne since the existing fire station was built.
Commercial occupancies and residential growth have exploded in the past 56 years, rendering
the existing station inadequate to ensure proper coverage to both commercial and residential
areas. Given the poor condition of Fire Station #5, Cheyenne’s Fire Chief, Greg Hoggatt, noted
“the only repairs that should be authorized are those that keep the station operational until the
new station can be built.”
The 56-year-old Fire Station was not built to accommodate the numerous advances in
firefighting, equipment and technology that now exist. A new station, to replace Station #5, will
provide for the care of firefighters, vehicles, and equipment. It will be built to provide all that is
needed for firefighting in 2020 and for many, many years to come.
A new station will provide firefighters with a station where the vehicles and equipment will be
properly stored and cared for within a temperature-controlled space. The existing station is not
well insulated, and the bays freeze, allowing the water pumps to freeze, which then makes them
unavailable for use. Temperature-controlled bays will keep the water pumps from freezing,
allowing for a fully functioning fire truck, ready to respond at a moment’s notice. The poor and
antiquated Station #5 HVAC system also has a negative effect on the firefighters who staff the
station. The reliability that a new station will afford firefighters, as well as the equipment and
vehicles, will benefit the public by the resulting faster response time and increased dependability.
A new Fire Station #5 will resolve the many issues with the existing station and will make
firefighting from Station #5 more efficient and reliable, if Fire Station #5 is not replaced, we are
nearing the point where the station will need to be abandon. At this point, this station can no
longer service the citizens of Cheyenne well.
In 2017, Cheyenne Fire and Rescue Chief, Greg Hoggatt, directed his staff to do a thorough
examination of the condition of each fire station and to submit a report to him. The chief
condensed the report and added his conclusions and recommendation into an executive summary
in October 2017, identifying Station #3 and #5 as needing replaced.
He prioritized Station #5 as
the most urgent of the two stating, “Begin the process to replace the station that will meet the
needs of the department in the future.”

form ny:
RESOLUTION_________
Date:Pi9I
ENTITLED: “A
RESOLUTION AUTHORIZING
SUBMISSION
OF
A MINERAL
ROYALTY
CAPITAL
CONSTRUCTION
ACCOUNT
GRANT
APPLICATION TO THE STATE LOAN AND INVESTMENT BOARD ON
BEHALF OF THE GOVERNING BODY OF THE CITY OF CHEYENNE
FOR THE PURPOSE OF REPLACING FIRE STATION #5.”
WHEREAS, the Governing Body of the City of Cheyenne (“City”) desires to participate
in the Mineral Royalty Capital Construction Account Grant program to assist in financing the
building of a new Fire Station #5; and
WhEREAS, the City recognizes the need for a new fire station as a replacement for the
existing antiquated Fire Station #5; and
WHEREAS, the Mineral Royalty Capital Construction Account Grant program requires
that certain criteria be met as described in the State Loan and Investment Board’s Rules governing
the program and, to the best of the City’s knowledge, this application meets those criteria; and
WHEREAS, grant funding for this Project will be considered by the State Loan and
Investment Board at its meeting on January 16, 2020; and
WHEREAS, if grant funding is awarded for this project, the Mayor is authorized to sign
the grant agreement with the State of Wyoming; and
WHEREAS, the total estimated Project cost is Four Million, Eight Hundred and Sixty-
Four Thousand, Seven Hundred and Ten Dollars ($4,864,710.00) and the City will request a grant
of half of this amount, or Two Million, Four Hundred and Thirty-Two Thousand, Three Hundred
and Fifty-Five Dollars ($2,432,355.00). The City plans to match the requested Mineral Royalty
Capital Construction Account Grant with Two Million, Four Hundred and Thirty-Two Thousand,
Three Hundred and Fifty-Five Dollars ($2,432,355.00) from the 5th and 6th Penny Sales Tax
Accounts.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED BY THE GOVERNING BODY OF THE
CITY OF CHEYENNE, that the City is authorized to submit a Mineral Royalty Capital
Construction Account Grant application for Two Million, Four Hundred and Thirty-Two
Thousand, Three Hundred and Fifty-Five Dollars ($2,432,355.00) to the State Loan and
Investment Board for consideration at the January 16, 2020 State Loan and Investment Board
meeting to assist in funding the building of a new Fire Station #5.
BE IT FURTHER RESOLVED, that the Mayor of the City of Cheyenne, is hereby
designated as the authorized representative of the City to act on behalf of the Governing Body on
all matters relating to this grant application.
PRESENTED, READ, AND ADOPTED THIS
DAY OF SEPTEMBER, 2019.
Marian J. Off, Mayor
(SEAL)
Attest:
Kristina F. Jones, City Clerk

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY:
This plat proposes replatting one large parcel into 23 single-family residential lots, 2 drainage lots, and 2
lots dedicated to the City of Cheyenne Greenway. The land is currently undeveloped.
PlanCheyenne is supportive of residential development at this location, and the proposed plat is able to
meet the intent and applicability of the MR Medium-density Residential zone district. This property, as
well as properties to the east, is designated Urban Residential by the Future Land Use Map in Plan
Cheyenne. The proposed residential use is considered a primary use for Urban Residential designated
areas. The proposed plat would not have an undue influence on the nearby properties.
The transportation network in this area of the community is previously developed. The plat proposes to
dedicate and construct approximately 600 linear feet of right-of-way as a new road to serve the proposed
development. The plat also proposes to remove the existing platted cul-de-sac at the end of Homestead
Avenue and proposes vacating 8-feet of the existing 24-foot wide alley adjacent to the north property
boundary. The vacation of the alley may requite a separate vacation ordinance as the alley was originally
platted as part of the Homestead Addition, First Filing Replat (see Condition 1(g)).
Twenty-three of the 27 lots are buildable. The remaining four lots would be reserved for drainage and
open space. Ownership and maintenance for the two unbuildable drainage lots (Lot 1, Block 1 and Lot 2,
Block 2) and the two open space lots (Lot 11, Block 1 and Lot 1, Block 2) is undear. Per Section 4.4.6 of
the UDC, ownership and maintenance of all open space shall be clearly defined on the final plat. The plat
currently notes that the lands are equally granted to the City of Cheyenne and to all owners of land
contained within the replat. The lots may be granted to the City or to the landowners within the replat
but may not be granted to both. Condition 1(a) of this plat map requires clarification of the dedication,
ownership and maintenance of the four previously noted lots.
This plat shows a street section and layout that do not meet street section requirements of the UDC
(Section 4.3.4). Approval of this preliminary plat does not approve the layout and design of the street,
sidewalks or tree lawn within the right-of-way. Conditions 1(c-e) of this plat map require specific revisions
of the right-of-way for the Final Plat in order to meet UDC standards.
With the staffrecommended conditions on the final Plat, this PrellminaiyPlat would meet
the review criteria set fwth in Section 2.12.d ofthe Cheyenne Unified Development Code,
therefore staffrecommends the Planning Commission recommendapproval ofthis
preilminaryplat, with the following condition:
1.
The Final Plat map shall:
a.
Define dedication, ownership and maintenance for:
o
Lot 1, Block 1
o
Lot 11, Block 1
o
Loti, Block2
o
Lot2, Block2
b.
Concurrent with the finalPlat submittal, provide a Drainage Study.
c.
PerSection 43.4 ofthe UDC, allright-of-way width shallbe a minimum 56-feet.
Revise right-of-way width between Lot 10, Block 1 andLot 8, Block 3.

PRELIMINARY PLAT DATA: In reviewing, providing comments, and making recommendations and
decisions on a proposed preliminary plat, the Staff, Planning Commission and City Council shall use the
following 8 criteria, as found in Section 2.1.2.d:
1. PLANCHEYENNE
- The application is in accordance with the developmentpolides andphysical patterns
identified/n PlanCheyenne.
PlanCheyenne is supportive of this proposed
preliminary plat.
Future Land Use Map
PlanCheyenne policies encourage infill development
within the Urban Service Boundary and the
maximization of existing infrastructure. The subject
property already has utilities readily available and is
within the Urban Service Boundary.
PlanCheyenne encourages the enhancement and
stabilization of existing residential neighborhoods and a
mix of housing types in new residential developments.
This proposed plat balances these two PlanCheyenne
policies by maintaining a low-density development
pattern but providing variation by proposing slightly
smaller lots than currently exist in the neighborhood
___________________________________
and by proposing attached housing units.
This property, and the area to the east, is designated
Urban Residential by the Future Land Use Map in PlanCheyenne. The proposed residential use is
considered a primary use for Urban Residential designated areas. The Urban Residential category
encourages a mix of housing types. Residential areas are generally encouraged to be 6-12 dwelling
units per acre for small lot single-family attached uses. The development proposed within the plat
boundary contains a density of about 61/2 dwelling units per acre, within PlanCheyenne’s expected
density range for a single-family neighborhood.
2. PLANNING
- The application reflects soundplanning and urban desi’n princ,oles with respect to
existing andpotential future development in the v/Unity, induding consideration ofcurrent and future
streets, open spaces, blocks and lots, andpublic utilities or facilities.
This application would create 27 total lots from previously undeveloped land in the MR Medium-
density Residential zone district. Properties to the east are currently zoned MR while properties to
the south are zoned P Public, properties to the west are zoned U Light Industrial, and properties to
the north are zoned U Light Industrial and CB Community Business. The residential properties
proposed with this plat would be of similar size compared to the nearby residential properties. Street
rights-of-way exist to the east, and this plat proposes to construct approximately 600 linear feet of

7

dedicated public right-of-way to serve the 27 proposed residential lots. Utility easements are provided
on the plat. This subdivision reflects sound principles considering current and future development in
the area and the plat proposed would not have an undue influence on the nearby properties.
3. SUBDIVISION STANDARDS - Division of/and meets the purposes, intent, and a//app//cable standards in
Art/de 4.
Transportation Network:
The plat proposes to dedicate and construct approximately 600 linear feet of public right-of-way
as well as two greenway connections. The proposed right-of-way would provide access to the
proposed residential development and could serve as a connection to existing neighborhoods to
the east and future developments to the north. The plat proposes to remove the existing platted
cul-de-sac at the end of Homestead Avenue and proposes vacating 8-feet of the existing 24-foot
wide alley adjacent to the north property boundary. The vacation of the alley may require a
separate vacation ordinance as the alley was originally platted as part of the Homestead Addition,
First Filing Replat (see Condition 1(g)).
The plat shows a street section and layout that do not meet street section requirements of the
UDC (Section 4.3.4). Approval of this plat does not approve the layout and design of the street,
sidewalks or tree lawn within the right-of-way (see Conditions 1(c-e).
Community facility Fees:
The land contained in this plat was previously platted as Center Point Subdivision in 2008. The
plat fees required in 2008 are the same as those currently required. Plat fees previously paid may
be credited toward those currently due (UDC 4.2.5.c). Assuming the original plat paid all
applicable fees, the total Land Acquisition & Infrastructure Fees due at signing of the plat is $0.
This plat proposes platting the subject property for residential development. The Residential
enhancement fee is $400 per dwelling unit.
Projected Residential Enhancement Fee
$9,200 ($400 per dwelling unit)
Per UDC Section 4.2.5, Table 4-2, Enhancement Fees are payable at the issuance of a
building permit for each structure.
TOTAL FEES DUE AT SIGNING OF THE PLAT: $0.
Open Space:
During the plat process, this property is not required to provide open space. Residential open
space is not required at time of platting on infill development of less than 10 acres, as long as
alternative open space options are available (UDC 4.4.3.b.3). The applicant has indicated the
intent to meet the requirements of on-site open space by providing access to the existing
Greenway and by dedicating two lots to the Greenway. The applicant has also indicated lands set
aside as unbuildable may effectively serve as open space available to these residents, however
these are noted as parcels dedicated to drainage. Drainage parcels generally do not fulfill open
space requirements (UDC 4.4.3.b). This plat generally meets the open space requirements.
Center Point Subdivision Preliminary Plat
I UDC-19-00391
8

Required Improvements:
This division can provide all required utilities to the properties within this development. However,
the Final Plat must show all BOPU easements (see Condition 1(f)).
4. PHASING
- Anyphasing proposed in the application is dearly indicated and demonstrates a logical and
well-planned developmentpattern. This may indude consideration ofamenities andpublic fadlities ofthe
subdivision within each phase compared to the anticipated timing or the likelihood offuture phases being
constructed as planned.
No specific phasing is proposed with this project.
5. Si1JrI1ES - The impacts identified in spedflc studies or technical reports that were prepared or
requested to be prepared in assodation with the application, and whether the findings and
recommendations ofthese reports, or any modifications or conditions on approval ofthe application, can
mitigate these impacts.
The applicant submitted a Drainage Worksheet, a Traffic Worksheet, and an Environmental Impact
Analysis. The Engineering Department did not find the Drainage Worksheet sufficient to address the
proposed project’s drainage. A Drainage Study is required with the final plat (see Condilion 1(b)).
The Engineering department did not identify any traffic volume issues which would require further
technical reports at time of final plat.
6. ZONING
- Allproposed lots shall be buildabie lots according
to the existing orproposed zoning district standards and
result/n site design and developmentpatterns that meet the
Intent andApplicabilitystatements ofthe exiting orproposed
zoning district. All parcels notproposed as buildable lots and
which are not dedicated to and accepted by the City shall have
a dearly defined entity responsible for ongoing maintenance.
This land is currently zone MR Medium-density Residential.
The MR zone is intended for a mix of density and dwelling
types within a neighborhood. The MR District is applicable
to areas where more compact neighborhood development
patterns are desired. The proposed project meets the
intent of the zone district by providing adequate access to
services and infrastructure.
The proposed plat contains 27 lots for a total twenty-two SD2 type (duplex) residential lots, one DD7
type (single-family detached) residential lot, 2 unbuildable lots dedicated to drainage, and two lots
dedicated to City greenway. The proposed SD2 type residential lots are medium-density style
development that fulfills the target density of 6-12 dwelling units per acre for the MR zone district.

The proposed single DD7 type lot is consistent with the existing low-density residential lot type
development to the east.
Ownership and maintenance for the two unbuildable drainage lots (Lot 1, Block 1 and Lot 2, Block 2)
and the two open space lots (Lot 11, Block 1 and Lot 1, Block 2) is unclear. Per Section 4.4.6 of the
UDC, all open space shall require documentation that demonstrates the ongoing maintenance and
management of the space. The plat currently notes that the lands are equally granted to the City of
Cheyenne and to all owners of land contained within the replat. The lots may be granted to the City
or to the landowners within the replat but may not be granted to both. Condition 1(a) of this plat
map requires clarification of the dedication, ownership and maintenance of the four previously noted
lots.
7. SURROUNDING DEVELOPMENT - The application will not create any undue influence on existing or
potential development for adjacentproperty considering the existing zoning and Comprehensive P/an
policies for thatproperty.
The plat proposes replatting a previously platted parcel in an area which has varying levels of
development. With the exception of one vacant parcel, properties to the north are developed with
commercial uses. The properties to the east are developed with residential uses. City greenway is
located directly to the south while F.E. Warren VFW Post 1881 is located directly to the west.
The subject parcel is adjacent to two existing street rights-of-way and proposes dedicating
approximately 600 linear feet of new right-of-way to serve the proposed single-family residences. As
rights-of-way can be used for access and utilities, the plat would not unduly influence the existing or
potential development on surrounding properties.
8. PUBLIC HEALTH, SAFETY, AND WELFARE
— Whether the application will harm the publlc health, safety or
general welfare.
With the recommended conditions on the Final Plat, Staff is satisfied the proposed preliminary plat
would not adversely affect the public health, safety, or general welfare.
